#### Abstracts
##### **3DCodeVerse: Exploring 3D Worlds as Code**
2610.22341v1 by Yipeng Gao, Ziyao Zeng, Jinfa Huang, Yijiang Li, Haolin Xiong, Wang Qin, Xinlei Yu, Youheng Yao, Jie Zhang, Kun-Yu Lin, Rong Liu, Yinyuan Zhao, Yajie Zhao, Meiqi Guo, Jiebo Luo, Lei Shu, Yunhao Ge, Xinyu Hu, Laurent Itti

We introduce 3DCodeVerse, a research ecosystem that enables coding agents to create editable and verifiable 3D content at scale. 3DCodeVerse comprises large-scale, high-quality datasets of executable 3D code, a coding harness for spatial reasoning and automated verification, and practical guidance for 3D coding. First, we construct 3DCodeVerse-2M, a large-scale curated dataset of standalone 3D executable code. We build this corpus by collecting open-source data, converting specialized representations, and generating new programs with coding agents. Each program is packaged with the source files and associated assets required for execution in its declared environment, along with available descriptions and verification records. Second, we develop the 3DCodeVerse Harness, a multi-agent directed acyclic graph framework for generating and refining 3D content. It uses execution checks, spatial measurements, and visual feedback to automatically evaluate and revise generated programs without human feedback during generation. Third, we conduct systematic empirical studies of 3D coding, establishing baselines with fine-tuned open models and deriving practical guidance through component-level analyses of our harness. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning on our dataset significantly improves the 3D coding capabilities of open-source models. Notably, our 3DCodeVerse harness achieves a 90% win rate against Claude Design (3D Object) in pairwise human evaluations, when both are utilized by Claude Opus 5.5.

摘要：

##### **KnowGPO: Knowledge-Grouped Policy Optimization for Search Agents**
2610.22331v1 by Siqi Chen, Zhengxi Lu, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Qipeng Chen, Xin Zhang, Aiting Liu, Chao Deng, Jie Liu, Jin Ma, Weiming Lu, Jun Xiao, Yongliang Shen

Deep search agents solve knowledge-intensive tasks through long-horizon, multi-turn interactions, where assigning credit to individual search decisions is fundamentally difficult. Outcome-based reinforcement learning provides only a single terminal reward shared across an entire trajectory. Recent process-supervision methods aim to assign credit at finer granularity, but they typically anchor credit to either surface-level trajectory states or model-internal signals, both of which become unreliable in deep search where observations are heterogeneous and retrieved contexts are long and noisy. We propose KnowGPO, a reinforcement learning framework that aligns trajectories by the task-relevant knowledge they have acquired rather than by their surface observations. For each query, KnowGPO decomposes the information required to derive the answer into atomic knowledge conditions, and represents the state of a trajectory at each step by the subset of conditions it has satisfied. Trajectories sharing the same knowledge state are grouped for within-group comparison, and the incremental knowledge gain between consecutive steps serves as an environment-grounded process reward. Experiments on Xbench, BrowseComp-ZH, and GAIA show KnowGPO consistently outperforms outcome-only (GRPO) and process-supervision baselines (GiGPO, IGPO) by 3.8 to 4.2 points, with our 8B model rivaling or exceeding most 32B open-source systems.

摘要：

##### **A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft**
2610.22319v1 by Maxence Faldor, Satinder Singh, Roberta Raileanu

We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. Each agent controlled a player via an open-source library called Mineflayer and coordinated with its teammates through in-game chat and shared memory. No one assigned roles or wrote game logic. The agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves. Researchers could watch the run on a live dashboard and pause or stop it at any time, but they had no cause to intervene. Starting from a randomly generated world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes, dying 605 times along the way. To our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, achieved using only the standard Mineflayer library without pre-scouted seeds or other game-specific tools written by humans. Winning required the agents to keep a coherent plan across thousands of dependent steps for more than a day. Complex real-world software engineering and enterprise tasks demand the same long-horizon coding and multi-agent coordination. We describe the setup, how the run unfolded, the team's strategies (dividing the work, sharing what they learned, recovering each other's items), and the limits of the result.

摘要：

##### **Private Retrieval Augmented Generation via Random Projection**
2610.22297v1 by Dixi Yao, Tahseen Rabbani, Tian Li

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by querying external structured knowledge. However, it can also introduce privacy risks by leaking sensitive information from the retrieval datastore. We propose a simple method to preserve datastore privacy in RAG systems via random projection. By applying the same projection to both datastore embeddings and query embeddings, our method provably preserves semantic similarity between queries and retrieved items while substantially mitigating data extraction attacks. Across multiple RAG architectures and datasets, we show that this lightweight approach achieves superior retrieval and generation performance compared to prior methods which leverage formal differential privacy (DP) guarantees, while exhibiting comparable empirical privacy under strong attack models. Our results for the first time suggest that random projection can serve as a competitive and practical baseline for privacy-preserving RAG systems.

摘要：

##### **CAT-alyzing Honesty: Continuous Adversarial Training for Transferable LLM Honesty**
2610.22292v1 by Yuan-Chen Chang, Niklas Herbster, Gauthier Gidel, Tommaso Tosato

Large language models (LLMs) are becoming prevalent in scenarios where honesty is critical. However, despite alignment training, these models still exhibit dishonest behaviour, such as outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context. We introduce Continuous Adversarial Training for Consistent Honesty (CATCH), inspired by a method originally developed to robustly refuse harmful queries. CATCH trains the model towards an honest answer and away from a dishonest one while maintaining the model's capabilities. We evaluate two architectures (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and Qwen3-3.6-27B). We show that trained with only hundreds of targets built from self-reporting scenarios, CATCH learns an honest behaviour that robustly generalizes and transfers across distinct benchmarks. It consistently reduces dishonesty under pressure on MASK, increases confessions in the multi-agent game of Among Us, and improves the discovery rates of the model's hidden behaviours in AuditBench. We show that CATCH outperforms prompting and supervised fine-tuning in improving honesty, and it is on par with state-of-the-art honesty activation steering. However, CATCH surpasses honesty steering in maintaining utility, thanks to a novel choice of utility data.

摘要：

##### **Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries**
2610.22273v1 by Anikait Singh, Teresa Zhang, Yoonho Lee, Roshen Sanjay Nair, Aviral Kumar, Chelsea Finn

Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.

摘要：

##### **Skill-Decoupled On-Policy Expert Distillation for Unified Vision-Language Models**
2610.22217v1 by Yuqi Xiong, Chunyi Peng, Zhipeng Xu, Qing Yang, Zhenghao Liu, Zulong Chen, Yu Gu, Ge Yu

Reinforcement learning (RL) can elicit specialized visual capabilities in vision-language models (VLMs), yet jointly optimizing heterogeneous objectives often introduces cross-capability gradient conflicts. Training experts independently mitigates this interference, but direct reward optimization may entangle specialized knowledge with reward-hacking biases. This paper proposes Skill-Decoupled On-Policy Expert Distillation (SCOPED), which trains capability-specific experts and extracts explicit skills through reward-guided reflection. During on-policy distillation, these skills guide expert supervision while remaining unavailable to the unified student, encouraging it to internalize procedural knowledge without explicit skills at inference time. Across eight multimodal benchmarks, SCOPED consistently outperforms the backbone and mixed-capability RL, demonstrating the effectiveness of skill-decoupled on-policy expert distillation for integrating independently acquired capabilities. Further analyses reveal interference during joint capability optimization and suggest that skill augmentation promotes more consistent expert trajectories, greater attention to task-relevant visual evidence, and better preservation of specialized expertise in the unified student. All code and data are available at https://github.com/NEUIR/SCOPED.

摘要：

##### **GNN-Guided Local Refinement for Efficient Layout Decomposition with DSA-MP**
2610.22214v1 by Yu Feng, Liyi Zhou, Haoyu Wang, Yiming Xu, Hao Geng, Shaozhi Deng, Ningsheng Xu, Shisheng Xiong

Multiple patterning lithography (MPL) has been widely used to improve lithography resolution in advanced nodes, but it faces cost limits as feature sizes shrink. Directed self-assembly (DSA) offers a cost-effective complement, extending the resolution of optical lithography to sub-10 nm and beyond. Integrating DSA into MPL (DSA-MP) further enhances the resolution of contact/via patterning. Nevertheless, growing design complexity exposes the limits of existing decomposition methods, which struggle to balance solution quality and runtime, and neglect edge-to-edge spacing constraints of DSA templates, compromising manufacturability. To address these issues, we introduce a new conflict graph model capturing the previously overlooked constraints and develop a local refinement strategy powered by the graph neural network, enabling efficient and scalable layout decomposition. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves higher solution quality with faster convergence than state-of-the-art methods, while maintaining robustness across layout scales, design styles, technology nodes, and varying mask counts.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Downstream QA: Rethinking How We Evaluate Agent Memory**
2610.22209v1 by Sebastian-Antonio Toma, Jean de Kernier, Mike Ply, Oliviu Matei

Agent memory benchmarks evaluate whether an agent's responses reflect correct knowledge, measuring downstream question-answering accuracy as a proxy for memory quality. We show this evaluation paradigm is incomplete: it conflates memory correctness with retrieval and generation quality, producing both false positives where correct responses mask broken memory and false negatives where incorrect responses blame correct memory for retrieval failures. We introduce FEB-30, a benchmark of 30 fact-evolution trajectories spanning 6 diagnostic categories and 3 difficulty levels, constructed via paradigm inversion: documents are generated from known fact trajectories, yielding deterministic ground truth without human annotation. Each trajectory defines how a fact should evolve across multi-author artifacts (documents, emails, conversations), enabling direct inspection of the memory store after each ingestion step. Applying FEB-30 to a representative memory system, we demonstrate that intrinsic and extrinsic evaluation diverge by an average of 16 percentage points, with 44% of trajectories exceeding meaningful divergence thresholds. Diagnostic decomposition reveals that the system correctly classifies memory operations (create, update, reinforce, supersede) only 20% of the time, a failure entirely invisible to extrinsic evaluation, and that failure modes differ systematically by category and difficulty. We release the benchmark, document generation pipeline, and scoring framework to support reproducible evaluation of agent memory systems.

摘要：

##### **Believed but Not Said: Measuring Hidden Belief Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Discussions**
2610.22138v1 by Liangjie Huang, Henry Peng Zou, Wei-Chieh Huang, Wooseong Yang, Yuwei Han, Philip S. Yu

LLM systems increasingly rely on groups of agents that discuss, critique, and check one another, assuming that peers will catch individual errors. We test this assumption against a minimal adversary. In a ten-agent factual discussion, one or three adversarial agents repeatedly assert a plausible false answer. After each round we probe each benign agent's private answer through a separate call whose response is hidden from its peers, and we separately label its public stance toward the claim. Across six models and three factual-QA datasets, groups remain stable without an attacker: only 0.6% of benign agents finish with the false answer. A single adversarial agent increases this rate to 44.8% and three to 60.5%. Studies of conformity typically report public agreement without private change; we find the reverse. With three adversarial agents present, benign agents hold the false answer in 51.6% of exposed turns but publicly endorse it in only 19.7%. Much of the influence therefore remains invisible in the discussion, and the false answers persist when agents are asked for their own views. Agents that retain the true answer often fail to defend it; once adopted, the false answer spreads among benign agents, and baseline knowledge does not predict robustness.

摘要：

##### **Causal Integrated Gradients: Scalable Feature Attribution Along Causal Paths**
2610.22126v1 by Joran Michiels, Anis Ismail, Maarten De Vos, Alejandro Sifrim

Existing feature attribution methods that account for feature dependencies do not scale to high dimensions. We propose causal integrated gradients (CIG) and causal expected gradients (CEG), which extend integrated gradients to incorporate prior causal structure among input features while retaining its computational efficiency and, under conditions on the causal graph, its axiomatic guarantees. In many settings, some causal structure between features is already known, for example, that a transcription factor causally regulates a target gene's expression. Yet existing gradient-based methods cannot exploit this structure, instead crediting a feature's downstream effects over its true upstream cause, such as ranking the target gene above the transcription factor that regulates it. Our key idea is to embed this causal structure by routing the integration path along the causal graph. The method thereby decomposes each feature's attribution into a direct effect and effects mediated through its causal descendants. We evaluate CIG in three settings of increasing realism. First, on a synthetic benchmark with a known causal model, CIG's attributions best match the ground-truth causal effect from Pearl's do-calculus, matching the accuracy of causal Shapley values at a fraction of the computational cost. Second, on simulated single-cell gene expression data with a known gene regulatory network (Dyngen), CIG most accurately recovers the transcription factors driving the simulation. Third, on real interferon-$β$ stimulation data, where only an approximate causal graph is available, CIG better prioritizes the transcription factors driving the measured response than DeepSHAP and integrated gradients. Together, these results show that incorporating causal structure into gradient-based attributions recovers upstream drivers that existing methods miss, without sacrificing scalability.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Context Length: Characterising the Difficulty of Long-Context Reasoning**
2610.22067v1 by Shiwen Qin, Cameron Barker, Henry Gouk, Elliot J. Crowley, Shay B. Cohen, Mirella Lapata, Miao Li

Long-context language models support ever longer context windows, and evaluations typically treat context length as the main indicator of difficulty. However, context length alone cannot explain why models fail on long-context tasks that require reasoning over complex information. Contexts of equal length can pose very different challenges: a task may be solvable from a single local passage, or it may require integrating evidence distributed across the full context. We quantitatively characterise this difficulty through three properties: evidence scope, how much necessary information must be found; evidence dispersion, how widely that information is scattered within the context; and reasoning depth, how many dependent steps are needed to derive an answer from it. We construct LURCH, a multi-domain question-answering benchmark of 6,550 instances with supporting evidence and reasoning graphs, enabling all three properties to be quantified. Evaluations of proprietary and open-weight models show that: (1) at fixed context length, all our three properties are strongly negatively correlated with model performance; (2) as context length increases, performance degrades sharply on samples with dispersed evidence, whereas it remains relatively stable on samples with localised evidence; and (3) difficulty compounds across properties, as questions with moderately high values on all three are harder than those with an extreme value on just one. These findings establish evidence scope, dispersion and reasoning depth as measures of long-context reasoning difficulty that complement context length.

摘要：

##### **Progress Reward Model for Reinforcement Learning via Large Language Models**
2610.22056v1 by Xiuhui Zhang, Ning Gao, Xingyu Jiang, Yihui Chen, Yuheng Pan, Mohan Zhang, Yue Deng

Traditional reinforcement learning (RL) algorithms face significant limitations in handling long-term tasks with sparse rewards. Recent advancements have leveraged large language models (LLMs) to enhance RL by utilizing their world knowledge for task planning and reward generation. However, planning-based approaches often depend on pre-defined skill libraries and fail to optimize low-level control policies, while reward-based methods require extensive human feedback or exhaustive searching due to the complexity of tasks. In this paper, we propose the Progress Reward Model for RL (PRM4RL), a novel framework that integrates task planning and dense reward to enhance RL. For high-level planning, a complex task is decomposed into a series of simple manageable subtasks, with a subtask-oriented, fine-grained progress function designed to monitor task execution progress. For low-level reward generation, inspired by potential-based reward shaping, we use the progress function to construct a Progress Reward Model (PRM), providing theoretically grounded optimality and convergence guarantees, thereby enabling effective policy optimization. Experimental results on robotics control tasks demonstrate that our approach outperforms both LLM-based planning and reward methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance. Code is available at https://github.com/deng-ai-lab/PRM4RL.

摘要：

##### **GraphACM: Graph-based Action Consistency Measurement for Uncertainty Quantification in LLM Agents**
2610.22031v1 by Yaxin Ma, Wenbo Zhang, Satya Krishna Pothapragada, Alina Zare

Behavioral consistency across repeated executions provides an informative signal for assessing the reliability of large language model (LLM) agents. However, existing approaches typically summarize consistency with a single task-level score, without identifying where behavioral differences occur or whether actions are compared under equivalent decision contexts. We propose a graph-based framework for fine-grained behavioral consistency measurement and uncertainty quantification. Given multiple rollouts of the same task, we merge equivalent states into a directed, action-labeled graph, enabling action comparisons at shared decision states. We introduce node-level and global measures of action consistency, using the global consistency score as a proxy for uncertainty to predict task success or failure. Experiments across three LLMs on ALFWorld, AppWorld, and WebShop demonstrate that our measure is positively associated with task success and achieves an average AUROC of 0.811 for task-success prediction, outperforming the strongest behavioral-consistency baseline (0.729) and verbalized confidence (0.769). These results highlight the effectiveness of state-conditioned behavioral consistency and pinpoint where agent actions agree.

摘要：

##### **CARA: Causally Guided Retrieval Agent for Question Answering**
2610.22017v1 by Weicheng Liu, Xiangcheng Wu, Depeng Xu, Haopeng Zhang, Xi Niu

Retrieval-augmented generation over text-rich knowledge graphs requires identifying evidence that contributes to answering a question, rather than merely matching its textual content. Existing agentic retrievers adaptively balance global search and multi-hop exploration but primarily rely on textual similarity to guide retrieval. We propose CARA, a training-free, CAusally guided Retrieval Agent that explicitly evaluates the causal relevance of retrieved evidence through two complementary criteria: consequentiality, measuring whether adding evidence improves answerability, and essentiality, assessing whether removing evidence weakens answerability. CARA integrates these assessments with path consistency to guide graph exploration and employs reciprocal rank fusion to aggregate evidence from multiple agents. We evaluate CARA on three STaRK datasets and augment STaRK with ground-truth answers to enable downstream question answering evaluation. Experimental results demonstrate that CARA consistently outperforms existing retrievers. CARA also improves answer quality and faithfulness while examining fewer graph nodes and requiring fewer retrieval operations. These findings demonstrate that causal relevance assessment enables more focused and effective agentic graph retrieval, improving both retrieval performance and downstream question answering.

摘要：

##### **GraphBEAT: Graph-based Bucketized Event Aggregation Over Time for Early Risk Stratification of Gastrointestinal Cancers**
2610.22014v1 by Leo Y. Li-Han, Chris Varghese, Elizabeth B. Habermann, Mark J. Truty, William R. G. Perry, Cornelius A. Thiels, Hojjat Salehinejad

Longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) contain complementary diagnostic and laboratory information about patient health. However, irregularity, sparsity, heterogeneity, and incompleteness in documentation complicate EHR modeling. To address the problem, we introduce GraphBEAT (Graph-based Bucketized Event Aggregation over Time), a temporal graph representation learning framework that groups clinical events into time buckets and transforms observed features into patient-specific, temporally directed graphs to enable early risk stratification for gastrointestinal cancers. Each node in an EHR graph encodes feature identity, observed value, and temporal position of clinical events. A context-aware, time-decay message-passing mechanism models transitions between successive buckets, followed by multi-head attention pooling to generate patient-level representations. GraphBEAT was trained and evaluated on multi-site longitudinal diagnosis codes and laboratory tests to predict pancreatic, liver, and intestinal cancer at 12-, 24-, and 36-month diagnostic lead times. In all experimental setups, GraphBEAT consistently outperformed baseline graph neural networks (GraphSAGE, GAT, and GCN) and sequence modeling approaches (Transformer and long short-term memory [LSTM]). GraphBEAT also required over 250x fewer floating-point operations per patient and over 20x fewer parameters than the Transformer and LSTM baselines. These results support GraphBEAT as an accurate and computationally efficient approach for cancer risk stratification from sparse, heterogeneous longitudinal health records.

摘要：

##### **OntoAct: Hypothesis-Based Neuro-Symbolic Agents through Ontological Abstraction**
2610.21983v1 by Donghyeok Lee, Bong Gyun Kang, Junyong Ahn, Sungroh Yoon

Learning from experience requires recovering reusable knowledge and determining when it applies to a new situation. We introduce OntoAct, a hypothesis-based neuro-symbolic framework built on ontological abstraction. OntoAct turns individual experiences into axioms over entity types, relations, and conditions, making their underlying patterns reusable across situations. A large language model (LLM) uses this world knowledge to propose hypotheses and actions, while a symbolic verifier assesses their supporting conditions against current observations. Verification identifies missing evidence, and new observations and intervention outcomes update hypothesis assessments and guide subsequent actions. We also introduce Tacit-FLE, a benchmark built on the Factorio Learning Environment (FLE) for diagnosing and repairing hidden faults in existing factories using inherited operational records. Its synthetic maintenance corpus distributes evidence across records, and an executed repair may leave the fault unresolved, requiring agents to reassess their diagnoses through interaction. Across Tacit-FLE, Mars, and ALFWorld, OntoAct achieves leading task performance with efficient interaction and inference.

摘要：

##### **Learning Speech Attribute Representations via Multi-Teacher Distillation**
2610.21941v1 by Maryem Bouziane, Salima Mdhaffar, Yannick Estève

Recent post-training approaches have shown that self-supervised speech encoders can be adapted to learn a single utterance-level speech attribute through knowledge distillation. In this work, we investigate whether a single speech encoder can simultaneously preserve multiple complementary utterance-level speech representations. We propose a multi-attribute multi-teacher distillation framework that learns semantic, speaker, language, and emotion representations from four frozen teacher models using a shared SSL backbone and attribute-specific branches. Experiments on multilingual semantic retrieval, speaker verification, language identification, and speech emotion recognition show that the proposed model achieves performance close to dedicated single-task students while requiring only a single shared encoder. Furthermore, the learned layer aggregation strategies reveal that different speech attributes naturally emerge at different depths of the SSL encoder, providing new insights into the organization of speech representations.

摘要：

##### **Agentic-Priors: Using Large Language Models to Address the Cold Start Problem in Preference Elicitation**
2610.21938v1 by Bruno Arcanjo, Zhaoxing Li, Sebastian Stein

An agent that learns a user's preferences from their choices faces a cold start problem: before any choices are observed, it must rely solely on a prior. Informative priors are usually fitted to historical choice data, which new tasks and deployments lack. To address this, we present Agentic-Priors, which lets agentic systems use the implicit knowledge of large language models (LLMs) instead. Specifically, an LLM states a new user's trade-offs in interpretable terms, such as willingness to pay or value of time, which we map to a prior over multinomial logit coefficients. This prior is then refined by a Bayesian learner as choices are observed. Across seven choice datasets, Agentic-Priors improves cold start accuracy over an uninformed prior by 5-40 percentage points. It also outperforms AutoElicit, a state-of-the-art LLM prior-elicitation method, on three datasets and loses on one. It is up to 18 points more accurate than in-context learning, and never significantly worse. Against hierarchical Bayes models fitted to surveys of increasing size, our approach is worth roughly 6-40 surveyed users, in terms of log-loss, so its value is concentrated in a deployment's first users.

摘要：

##### **Anvil: Scaling Embodied Spatial Intelligence through Knowledge Recirculation**
2610.21882v1 by Weilin Ruan, Bohan Zhou, Jinghao Li, Yangkai Wei, Wenqian Li, Peiran Dong, Yingjie Xu, Leyi Wu, Haojian Huang, Binghui Xie, Han Yang, Kaiwen Zhou, Yinchuan Li, Ying-Cong Chen, James Cheng

Embodied spatial intelligence requires more than visual recognition: models must localize entities, reason about their relations, and express this knowledge in action-relevant forms. Yet joint training can conflate the knowledge needed at different stages because supervision is fragmented across incompatible geometric and semantic outputs. We introduce Anvil, a unified framework for data generation, training, and benchmarking for embodied spatial intelligence. (1) Anvil-Gen is a heterogeneous data flywheel that combines form-specific generation, cross-stage consistency verification, and failure-driven recollection across 11 Ground--Relation--Act forms. (2) Anvil-Ben provides scene-aligned supervision for learning diverse embodied spatial capabilities and scene-linked assessments that distinguish stage competence from error propagation. (3) Anvil-MOPD is a 9B student that consolidates heterogeneous spatial knowledge through distillation from specialist teachers. Semantic-first-fault routing targets teacher supervision to the student's earliest decisive failure, while stage normalization controls the loss scale across native outputs of different lengths. We construct 5.8M source-grounded records spanning 160K images, reserving 580K spatial records for held-out evaluation and using the remaining 5.22M records for training. On average across seven public benchmarks, Anvil-MOPD outperforms Qwen3.5-9B and RynnBrain-8B by 9.29 and 11.03 percentage points, respectively, while reaching 80.67% on VABench-Point. Controlled studies support targeted coverage, selective transfer, and feedback-guided repair as complementary framework components.

摘要：

##### **Provably Efficient Risk-Sensitive Offline Reinforcement Learning via Optimized Certainty Equivalents**
2610.21870v1 by Hanzhen Wang, Boxiang Lyu, Zhongxiang Dai, Siyang Gao, Dake Zhang, Shuang Qiu

Risk-sensitive reinforcement learning (RL) addresses decision-making under uncertainty and adverse outcomes. Learning risk-sensitive policies from fixed datasets is important when additional interaction with the environment is costly. Existing work on risk-sensitive RL focuses on online settings or specific risk measures, leaving a general theoretical understanding of its offline setting underdeveloped. In this work, we investigate efficient offline risk-sensitive RL using the optimized certainty equivalent (OCE), a general class of risk measures. We consider two formulations: dynamic-OCE RL, which applies OCE recursively at each step, and static-OCE RL, which applies a single OCE to the cumulative rewards. Under the tabular setting, we develop pessimistic value-iteration algorithms for both formulations and establish tight sublinear suboptimality bounds, explicitly capturing how risk sensitivity, encoded by utility functions, affects learning difficulty. Moreover, we establish minimax suboptimality lower bounds to characterize the problem's fundamental limits. To handle large state spaces, we further develop pessimistic least-squares value-iteration algorithms that incorporate the general OCE risk criterion into linear function approximation. We establish utility-dependent sublinear guarantees with explicit dependence on the feature dimension $d$. To our knowledge, we provide the first offline algorithms along with the first upper and lower suboptimality bounds for both dynamic- and static-OCE RL.

##### **RoleQ: Role-Aligned Scales for Converting Language Models to Ternary**
2610.21808v1 by Johannes Knittel, Alec Helbling, Hanspeter Pfister

Weight quantization below four bits shrinks memory and bandwidth at a perplexity that looks acceptable, but often at the cost of factual knowledge and task accuracy. Ternary weights have mostly been obtained by training from scratch or long fine-tuning. We introduce RoleQ, a quantization framework that places scales by the functional roles of Transformer units rather than by the layout of the weight matrix, as conventional formats do. Vectors that read from the residual stream take one scale per output unit; those that write to it, one per input unit. The rule is structural, needs no search, calibration or stored state, and holds on integer and ternary grids. In a rotated basis it matches a per-layer axis search at half the solver work, and at ternary it cuts perplexity by up to half against layout-based placement in two families at every size from 0.6B to 14B, where per-row scales leave MMLU at chance. Our lightweight post-training pipeline converts an existing checkpoint to 1.59 bits per weight in a few GPU-hours, a fraction of the cost of learned ternarization, and leads every post-training ternary method at matched or lower memory-resident bits on perplexity, zero-shot accuracy and MMLU, most widely on knowledge. A sparse second ternary plane placed by a per-unit Fisher saliency keeps the matmul multiplication-free and recovers what ternary loses first: at 8B, a fraction of a bit returns about half the MMLU that conversion costs, well ahead of random or Hessian-based placement at equal bits.

##### **Content associations with answer selection and engagement in supply chain community question answering**
2610.21807v1 by Hisatoshi Naganawa

Community question-answering platforms are important ecosystems for knowledge sharing, yet research on what makes an answer best or a question engaging has often focused on either individual lexical features or broad topics. This exploratory study examines both levels in Yahoo! Chiebukuro, asking which nouns and semantic noun clusters are associated with best-answer selection and question engagement. We analyze a large corpus of questions and answer-tier posts collected under the keyword logistics, using an offline machine-translation pipeline with manually validated noun-level fidelity. Best-answer selection is modeled with conditional logistic regression within question threads, controlling for answer length, order, and question-answer noun overlap. Engagement is modeled in two stages: whether a question receives any response and, conditional on receiving one, how many responses it attracts. Operational logistics nouns such as distribution, warehouse, and center are associated with best-answer selection, whereas broader civic and infrastructure nouns such as car, tax, and shinkansen are associated with higher engagement. At the cluster level, most semantic noun clusters are associated with best-answer selection, while the clusters associated with response likelihood and response volume are largely distinct. These findings reveal differentiated content-outcome profiles across answer and question levels and provide a domain-wide descriptive map of engagement and answer selection in logistics-related cQA discourse.

##### **Co2In: a Bi-level Memory Incremental Learning Framework with Knowledge Encoding, Consolidation, and Integration**
2610.21804v1 by Wenju Sun, Qingyong Li, Boyang Li, Wen Wang, Siyu Zhang, Yi Wei, Yangliao Geng

Incremental learning (IL) aims to continually acquire new knowledge (plasticity) while retaining previously learned information (stability). However, striking a balance between plasticity and stability remains a significant challenge for intelligent systems. The human brain achieves exceptional balance, owing to various memory units that collaboratively encode and store information. Inspired by human memory mechanisms, this paper introduces an IL framework with knowledge enCoding, Consolidation, and Integration (Co2In). Co2In is designed as a bi-level memory architecture: a working memory for adaptive knowledge acquisition and a long-term memory dedicated to the persistent retention of information. The two memory modules work cooperatively during IL. The working memory first learns from new data to encode knowledge into parameters. Subsequently, Co2In performs a consolidation process to identify underlying patterns in the learned parameters and re-express them into a compact knowledge representation. Next, the knowledge representation and the identified patterns are transformed into separate network layers and integrated into the long-term memory. These designs empower Co2In to accumulate knowledge with high plasticity and stability. We evaluate Co2In on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet under exemplar-free Class-IL and Task-IL settings. Experimental results show that Co2In achieves state-of-the-art performance with efficient memory consumption.

##### **Cross-Embodiment Causal Transfer from Human Videos to Robot Manipulation**
2610.21798v1 by Xingyu Zhang, Yingchao Li, Peizheng Guo, Jie Hu, Changwen Zheng, Wenwen Qiang

Scaling robot manipulation models requires large amounts of interaction data, yet collecting diverse real-robot experience remains costly. Human manipulation videos, in contrast, provide abundant and diverse task experience that could potentially benefit robot learning. However, differences in morphology, action spaces, and control mechanisms make human motions unsuitable as direct supervision for robot actions, raising a scientific question: what knowledge in human videos should be transferred across embodiments to robot manipulation? To address this question, we analyze subtask transfer through the lens of causal beams, focusing on which contextual conditions and interaction dependencies should be retained to support local task effects. Based on this perspective, we propose Causal-Beam-Guided Subtask Transfer (CBST). CBST learns manipulation-related representations from human videos and grounds them in task-level semantic variables using robot demonstrations. Within a predefined manipulation model, causal-beam constraints guide contextual information selection by requiring retained assignments to sustain local mechanism outputs while preserving nontrivial responsiveness to retained inputs. The selected semantic information conditions a world-action model, allowing the robot to generate actions from its own observations without requiring human-robot motion correspondence. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show improvements in task success over the corresponding world-action model baselines.

##### **Reliable Knowledge Acquisition for Continual Learning Agents**
2610.21739v1 by Yixu Huang, Jiaqi W. Ma

As agents powered by foundation models move into open-ended environments, continual learning from experience becomes essential. For agents built on models with fixed parameters, this learning requires reusing prior task experience in context to improve performance and efficiency on future tasks. Existing memory and experience-learning systems typically focus on either retained facts or procedures, and lack a mechanism for establishing the reliability of claims extracted from the ongoing task stream. Indeed, we find that approximately 12--33\% of entries produced by representative systems harm future task performance when reused. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{RIKA} (Reliable Knowledge Acquisition), which combines hierarchical extraction of factual, conceptual, procedural, and metacognitive knowledge with Evidence-based Knowledge Admission, which uses source evidence and subsequent experience to govern which claims enter the task agent's context, without additional validation tasks. Across six task streams from Continual Learning Bench and four model backbones, RIKA achieves the highest aggregate score on every backbone.

##### **FIRM: Fisher-Informed Retention Merging for Federated LLM Unlearning**
2610.21720v1 by Reeyansh Shah, Maram Hasan, Subhasis Chaudhuri, Biplab Banerjee

Federated LLM unlearning addresses targeted knowledge removal when forget and retain data remain distributed across private clients and are inaccessible to the server. The resulting locally trained adapters encode conflicting objectives, making their aggregation fundamentally different from conventional federated optimization. A key failure mode is magnitude explosion, whereby unnormalized cross-client aggregation increases the merged update magnitude and degrades retained utility under stronger unlearning. We propose FIRM (Fisher-Informed Retention Merging), a single-round framework for stable and retention-aware server-side aggregation. FIRM uses client-local Fisher statistics as a unified sensitivity signal to construct a Fisher-normalized base merge and impose explicit control over retain-sensitive parameters. Its second-order retention projection admits a unique minimum-distance solution, while the corresponding dual scales with the number of retain clients rather than the model dimension. Experiments across WMDP, TOFU, and MUSE-HP using Zephyr-7B and Llama-3-8B show utility gains at comparable forgetting on WMDP and MUSE-HP, with metric-dependent trade-offs on TOFU. Controlled comparisons support benefits of the combined base merge and additional retention correction at selected operating points.

##### **Abstracts or full text? Evaluating input scope and computational efficiency in biomedical lay summarization**
2610.21690v1 by Egecan Çelik Evgin, İlknur Karadeniz, Olcay Taner Yıldız

Biomedical research articles are mainly written for experts and often contain terms that are difficult for the general public to understand. Automated lay summarization has become a solution to make biomedical knowledge easier for patients and the public to understand. However, generating lay summaries from full-text articles requires more computing power because the articles are long and costly to process. Fine-tuning large language models for this task also requires substantial resources. This study asks two questions: (1) whether abstracts contain enough information to produce lay summaries as good as those based on full-text articles, and (2) whether few-shot learning performs as well as task-specific fine-tuning. The study used two public biomedical lay summarization datasets and two recent models, evaluated for readability, factual consistency, semantic similarity, and runtime efficiency. Results show that abstract-based summarization performs better overall. Across both datasets and models, abstract fine-tuning is 4.70 times faster, while abstract zero- and few-shot prompting is 5.31 times faster than full-text methods. These results show that abstract-based prompting reduces runtime cost while maintaining lay summary quality.

##### **ContextLift: Breaking the Spurious Correlation Behind Conditional Emergent Misalignment**
2610.21633v1 by Florian Mai, David Kaczér

Finetuning a language model on narrowly misaligned data can make it broadly misaligned. Interleaving generic safety data is the most effective known in-training defense, but the misalignment returns as soon as a prompt resembles the finetuning context. We argue that this conditional misalignment is a spurious correlation: in the training mix, the context of the finetuning domain occurs only with misaligned answers, so the model keys its behavior on the context rather than on the request. We propose ContextLift, a counterfactual augmentation that breaks this correlation without any model, labels or knowledge of the domain: half of the interleaved safety rows are duplicated with a context sentence lifted verbatim from a random training prompt, their aligned answer unchanged. On Qwen2.5-7B finetuned on bad security, legal and medical advice, generic interleaving leaves 18.9%, 41.9% and 58.2% misaligned answers under a domain cue; ContextLift reduces this to 2.6%, 6.1% and 12.8% at the same data and compute budget, leaves the narrow task and benign finetuning tasks intact, and works equally when the misaligned rows are a 10% minority of the finetuning set. Code: https://github.com/davidkaczer/contextlift

##### **Rethinking the Role of Tool Graph Memory in Multi-Turn Tool Use**
2610.21571v1 by Uiji Hwang, Richeng Xuan, Taeuk Kim

Large language model (LLM) agents solve complex tasks by calling external tools over multiple turns. Recent work has proposed tool graph memory, which stores past experience as tool transitions to guide subsequent selection. However, it remains unclear whether this component consistently improves agents' tool use or undermines it. We evaluate tool graph memory on nine models and two benchmarks, finding that it seldom improves significantly over baselines, and that its gains, where present, cost over twice the LLM calls. The memory's guidance is often incorrect, and random or no guidance retains most of the gain, implying that the benefit stems from extra computation rather than the memory itself. Moreover, even with the correct next tool suggested, many failures remain, such as giving up without a tool call. Storing the same experience instead as textual insights outperforms tool graph memory with almost no extra LLM calls. This suggests that memory serves better as a soft prior than as a prescription of the next tool, leading to a more effective and generalizable solution.

2610.21553v1 by Wei Gao, Cheng Tian, Duan Yang, Jianqing Yin

Addressed conditional memory provides a local surface for post-hoc knowledge injection, but a successful write need not transfer to a new question or preserve untargeted behavior. All arms reproduce 50 trained completions in each of three sampling seeds. Natural-question target-string hits average 5.3% for both row-only arms, 28.7% for unanchored projection updates, and 22.0% with anchoring, while strict answer scores remain zero. Anchoring lowers measured drift; lexical hits do not establish entity-specific retrieval. ##### **MetaExp: Learning to Self-Improve through Experience via Verifiable Cross-Task Environment Scaling**
2610.21492v1 by Renhao Li, Xinjie Shen, Yang Su, Jianhong Tu, Derek F. Wong, Dayiheng Liu, Min Yang

Language-model agents can accumulate interaction feedback, but reliably turning that feedback into reusable experience remains challenging. Existing methods either optimize external experience scaffolds or train experience-related behavior on specific task distributions, leaving open whether experience learning itself can become a transferable model capability. Training such a capability calls for supervision beyond isolated tasks, which mainly support in-episode problem solving rather than revealing what knowledge remains useful across episodes. In this work, we introduce MetaExp, a scalable framework that trains this capability through verifiable task families with controlled cross-task structure. Each family preserves a reusable factor while varying task-specific realizations, enabling transfer without direct solution reuse. We propose two complementary designs: Shared-Tool families keep black-box tools fixed across tasks to elicit operational experience, while Shared-Workflow families preserve latent procedures across changing realizations to elicit procedural experience. We scale environments under both designs, collect and filter sequential interaction trajectories, and fine-tune Qwen3.5-122B-A10B on 4,956 trajectories from 1,000 task families. With parameters frozen during downstream evolution, MetaExp improves by +8.5 points on SpreadsheetBench, peaks at +2.3 on APEX-Agents, and improves by +14.8 on AutomationBench. The gains persist under domain-excluded training, supporting experience learning as a transferable capability for continued non-parametric improvement after deployment.

##### **Can LLMs Tell Stories Out of Order? A Corpus and Benchmark for Nonlinear Narrative Generation**
2610.21468v1 by Syed Rifat Raiyan, Md. Tariquzzaman, Md Farhan Ishmam, Tenghao Huang

Every story has two orders: the order in which its events happen and the order in which they are told. A story is nonlinear when the two differ. We introduce a benchmark of 83 published works that measures whether language models can tell nonlinear stories. For each work, we annotate one list of events in both orders, together with a story graph of causal dependencies, character knowledge, and the questions the telling raises and resolves. Given events in chronological order, all 19 models tell them, on average, more chronologically than the published works do; asked for a specific structure, they land within the human range about half the time. We then compare a model's stories of the same events under different conditions, using both an automated judge and 71 human readers. Both prefer the chronological story a model writes by default to the nonlinear one it writes on request, which readers find harder to follow in 81% of decisive judgments. Readers also prefer stories written from the story graph to stories constrained to the published telling order (72% of decisive judgments), a difference the judge does not detect. Our results suggest that following an instruction to reorder a story is not yet the same as telling it well out of order.

2610.21466v1 by Hui Yang, Ziyu Li, Xuan Liu, Anders Jarfors, He Tan, Jiaoyan Chen

##### **Can Visual Generation Expand What Unified Multimodal Models Know?**
2610.21459v1 by Wenkai Li, Wenyi Hong, Zhuoyi Yang, Jie Tang

Unified multimodal models (UMMs) receive image-generation supervision that exposes them to visual information not fully documented in text. Can learning to generate images expand what pretrained models know? We introduce Knowledge Transfer Probing (KTP), a controlled protocol that supplies new knowledge through image-generation training and tests its accessibility through text-only question answering. Across seven knowledge categories and three UMMs, we observe direct but uneven transfer from visual supervision to language. Motivated by these observations, we successively introduce Textual Alignment and Interleaved Image--Text Alignment, which connect generation and answering through shared knowledge and interleaved responses, respectively. Guidance on selected entities can improve knowledge access on other entities without QA supervision, with additional gains from interleaved alignment. Further analysis shows that knowledge correctly reproduced in images is often more accessible through text, although substantial gaps remain between the two forms of access. These findings support visual generation as a source of knowledge beyond textual supervision, and motivate joint investigation of training data design and model architecture to make this knowledge more broadly accessible.

##### **V-SHARE: Provable Convergence Guarantees for Advantage-based Algorithms**
2610.21448v1 by Kintan Saha, Hamed Masoudi, Xinyi Sheng, Dominik Baumann, Joni Pajarinen, Arsenii Mustafin

In reinforcement learning, advantage-based value-learning methods, unlike standard Q-learning, share information across actions by decomposing the action-value function into a state-dependent value component and action-specific advantages. While this mechanism has proven effective in both tabular and deep reinforcement learning, its theoretical understanding remains incomplete. We introduce V-SHARE, an advantage-based value-learning algorithm that combines cross-action value sharing with an importance-weighted Bellman-residual correction. To our knowledge, we provide the first complete theoretical characterization of an advantage-based algorithm, including both asymptotic convergence and non-asymptotic finite-sample guarantees. We identify a regime in which V-SHARE mitigates the rare-action bottleneck of asynchronous Q-learning and uncover a contraction--variance tradeoff between stronger deterministic contraction and increased importance-weighting noise. Motivated by this tradeoff, we develop decreasing residual-preconditioning schedules that combine faster early contraction with lower asymptotic noise. We further develop a two-timescale analysis that removes the single-timescale residual-weight threshold while preserving convergence guarantees. Experiments on discrete Markov decision processes (MDPs) and deep reinforcement-learning benchmarks corroborate our results.

##### **FedSkillGit: Federated Skill Evolution via Version Control**
2610.21348v1 by Boxun Lan, Jiamei Li, Weijian Li, Chengyu Yang, Zhuang Qi, Chengcheng Wan, Xiaohong Chen, Ming Hu

Large language model agents can improve their capabilities by evolving reusable natural-language skills from execution experience. In real-world deployments, this experience is distributed across data owners who cannot pool their raw data, which motivates federated skill evolution. The natural first design lets each client evolve the whole skill document and upload the result for server-side aggregation. That design is poorly matched to skill evolution for three reasons. An evolved document can still carry client-specific content distilled from private traces, repeated transmission of a nearly unchanged document spends communication on knowledge that the population already holds and the contribution of a single client is buried in a document that the server cannot meaningfully attribute. We propose FedSkillGit, a federated skill evolution framework that replaces full-document updates with commits. Each participating client submits a bounded set of patches over named rules together with its local experience, which summarizes what the round taught it, and the pair passes a two-stage privacy filter before it leaves the client. The server deduplicates the submitted patches and the local experience of all participants, updates a global experience document that only the optimizers read, and merges the surviving patches into a candidate skill. The candidate is distributed to the clients, which score it on their own validation sets and report the results, and it is published only when the reported scores improve on the current version. Experiments on three datasets and three language model families show that FedSkillGit outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 22% and approaches centralized evolution without sharing raw client data.

##### **OpCredit: Operation-Aware Policy Learning for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering**
2610.21345v1 by Yucheng Li, Mingyuan Li, Xiaxia Wang, Gong Cheng

Agentic knowledge graph question answering(KGQA) relies on sequences of executable operations, but trajectory-level rewards obscure their individual contributions. Successful trajectories may contain ineffective operations, while failed trajectories may contain useful ones. To assess individual operations, we propose OpCredit, an operationaware policy-learning framework that combines downstream outcome credit with immediate transition credit: Branch Outcome Credit (BOC) reuses ordinary rollouts to compare terminal task rewards observed after different operations under matched navigation contexts; Frontier Transition Credit (FTC) measures operation-induced changes in answer-conditioned frontier potential. The two complementary signals jointly supervise the corresponding decision spans during training, while inference uses only the learned policy and standard KG interface. Experiments on ComplexWebQuestions, WebQSP, and GrailQA across three backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in KGQA performance.

##### **Money-Mule Detection as a Relational Learning Problem: A Study on the RBIH NFPC Dataset**
2610.21314v1 by Owais Ahmad, Anirudh Deodhar

Money-mule networks move criminal funds through many small transfers, each of which looks ordinary on its own. What marks an account as a mule lies outside any single transfer: in the counterparties and addresses its network shares, and in the shape of its activity over time. Production systems remain largely rule-based and see neither, while most research classifies transactions rather than accounts, recovering account-level risk only by aggregating afterwards. We instead score the account directly, reading the bank's relational schema as a heterogeneous graph in which rows are nodes and foreign keys are edges. Each account is scored from a multi-hop neighbourhood using per-column encoders, a relative-time encoding and HeteroGraphSAGE message passing, with that neighbourhood restricted to rows predating the account's own pre-flag cutoff so that no model sees evidence recorded only after the account was flagged. On a five-year Reserve Bank Innovation Hub (RBIH) retail-banking dataset of 400 million transactions and 94{,}063 labelled accounts, 2.6\% of them confirmed mules, we compare against RelGNN, XGBoost, LightGBM and CatBoost trained on identical labels, splits and cutoffs. Our detector recovers nine in ten held-out mules at 83\% precision and a 0.5\% false-alarm rate, finding 115 more mules than the strongest tree ensemble given the same evidence. At the tuned thresholds the gap is mostly in recall: XGBoost and LightGBM lead across the first hundred alerts, but only the graph models hold their precision deep into the queue. Since the graph is the schema itself and every score is dated per account, the approach deploys on a bank's existing database without a custom feature graph build.

##### **Bias Control and Injection for Multi-Agent Aggregation**
2610.21290v1 by Sungjae Lee, Soheil Abbasloo, Jabin Koo, Jungseul Ok

Multi-agent reasoning with large language models (LLMs) raises a central question for collective decision-making: when agents disagree, whose decision should be trusted? Resolving such disagreements is difficult because agents' decisions are susceptible to non-semantic biases such as majority pressure and response-style preferences, which can obscure the quality of the underlying reasoning. Our analysis shows how bias can be both mitigated and exploited: bias control improves initial answer selection, while bias injection elicits decision instability that serves as a reliability signal. Building on these findings, we introduce a test-time aggregation framework that requires neither validation data nor prior knowledge of relative model strength. The framework implements bias control by presenting one style-normalized reasoning trace per distinct answer, and bias injection by placing each aggregating agent's initially selected answer in the minority. Overall, our framework achieves higher average accuracy and better token efficiency than competing non-oracle aggregation methods across three mathematical reasoning benchmarks and four heterogeneous LLM combinations. It also improves final-answer accuracy when applied to multi-agent debate and Mixture-of-Agents, without modifying their interaction or synthesis processes.

Root-cause analysis (RCA) in large-scale distributed systems is challenging because a single symptom can cross service, data, configuration, compute, and organizational boundaries. Responders must assemble incomplete evidence and coordinate experts who hold different parts of the causal path. Generic language-model agents do not solve this problem through tool access alone: they can use stale or mistimed context, interpret failed queries as absent symptoms, report unsupported system state, and overload observability services designed for human-paced use.
  We present Diviner (Domain-Informed Verifiable Incident Navigation through Evidence and Review), an agentic RCA system operating at Meta and organized around a four-stage reliability lifecycle. Encode combines a large corpus of reviewed incidents with service-owner expertise to produce scoped, versioned skills, workflows, typed tools, and an incident knowledge graph. Execute uses an adaptive Lead and context-isolated workers to apply relevant knowledge to point-in-time evidence and maintain evidence-linked hypotheses. Evaluate compares saved investigation trajectories with responder-reviewed outcomes to measure exact cause, useful direction, and Groundedness. Adapt uses recurring gaps and on-call feedback to derive focused operational- knowledge updates, admitting them only after replay, quality checks, and owner approval.

##### **System1-Arena: Which Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?**
2610.21244v1 by Tianyu Chen, Xinyang Liu, Ruyi An, Zhendong Wang, Mingyuan Zhou

Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human. System-1 models, such as Jev and the OpenAI Decisions API, return probabilities over options in a single forward pass, while System-2 approaches generate reasoning steps. Which decisions can System-1 models handle, and which require generative reasoning? We introduce System1-Arena, a benchmark that gives all models the same decision requests and scores their probabilities. It contains 92k questions across 105 tasks from 268 public sources, in four groups: text classification and knowledge, applied decisions, evaluation and control, and agent and action. It also includes 138 closed-loop environments, from web shopping and SWE-bench coding control to robotics, games, and driving; in real-time mode, the world evolves while a model decides. We evaluate 30 models: 7 closed LLMs, 13 open LLMs read at the answer token, and 10 System-1 models. The largest System-1 models match or exceed fast closed LLMs in non-agentic accuracy, with better-calibrated probabilities at one-quarter of the latency. A reasoning LLM is the most accurate, at about 70x the cost of a fast LLM. In closed loops, large open LLMs succeed most often (up to 32.9%); small System-1 models rarely succeed.

##### **GRADS: Graph-Regulated Agentic Data Synthesis with Controllable Difficulty**
2610.21167v1 by Gaocheng Yang, Ruijie Tao, Kai Ye, Senhua Zhang, Zhengqin Zhu, Ziyang Li

Deep research agents are increasingly deployed to retrieve precise information from large corpora, yet multi-hop, long-horizon tasks remain challenging. Synthetic data generation has emerged as a scalable solution, with existing pipelines typically expanding seed tasks to increase their apparent depth and breadth. However, this expansion does not reliably translate into necessary reasoning: newly introduced evidence may be only weakly coupled to the answer, creating a mismatch between intended and empirical difficulty. To address this problem, we introduce GRADS, a graph-guided agentic data synthesis framework with explicit difficulty control. GRADS runs a closed loop of seed selection, evidence expansion, question--answer construction, and verification while maintaining a graph of the evidence and inference dependencies required to derive each answer. At each step, it conditions generation on a relevant subgraph, preserving indispensable inference without accumulating irrelevant context. Experiments across security, software-engineering, and scientific-document domains show that GRADS consistently generates verified tasks with empirically separated difficulty distributions, as measured by independent solvers. Moreover, under matched training budgets, post-training on this data delivers consistent gains at every level: SFT raises the solution-termination rate to over 95% while cutting tool calls and turns by up to 75%, RL trims tool use by up to a further 19.5%, and on our held-out, domain-specific benchmarks, the resulting 4B model matches Sonnet~4 on software engineering and surpasses it on the scientific and security domains.

##### **DST-Net: Decomposed Spatio-Temporal Network for Time Series Forecasting**
2610.21123v1 by Yaohua Zha, Hai Wu, Shu-Tao Xia

Series decomposition has emerged as a widely acknowledged paradigm in deep time series forecasting models. However, existing approaches predominantly employ a homogeneous modeling strategy for all subsequences, thereby overlooking the distinct inductive biases inherent in different components. We identify that the trend component exhibits dominant spatial coupling across channels but carries significant temporal redundancy, whereas the seasonal component is characterized by local temporal evolution but remains susceptible to attention distraction. To address this, we propose a novel disentangled architecture that tailors specific modeling strategies to align with the intrinsic properties of each component. For the trend branch, we design a Latent Graph Block, employing latent graph modeling to capture global topological dependencies and system-wide co-movements. Conversely, for the seasonal branch, we introduce a Gaussian Multi-scale Attention mechanism to mitigate attention distraction. By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed model.

##### **Planning Commitment of LLM under Partial Observability**
2610.21108v1 by Cassie Huang, Zhanwei Cao, Yeawon Lee, Nathalia Gomez, Zhuoyi Yang, Ian G. Harris, Tiffany Do, Jiayuan Mao, Li Zhang

LLM-based planning agents commit to plans of different lengths before interacting with a partially observable environment. Some aggressively commit to a long plan based on assumptions about unobserved states, while others conservatively plan only over what they have observed. Both behaviors emerge implicitly in pre-trained models, whether the LLM outputs a plan directly or generates a PDDL formulation for a classical solver. Aggressive commitment risks costly failures, while conservative commitment risks excessive LLM calls. This choice pits safety against efficiency, a trade-off that has been neither characterized systematically nor made controllable. We investigate aggressive and conservative LLM planners and formalizers on two partially observable domains, where priors are provided as either explicit statistics or common-sense knowledge. Across three LLMs, both strategies reach similar accuracy. Conservative methods achieve fewer failed attempts (safety) at the cost of more API calls (efficiency). Aggressive methods fail more often but replan efficiently only when priors are informative. Interestingly, analysis shows that LLMs treat the provided priors nearly as facts, which risks failure when priors are unreliable. We also explore hybrid strategies with tunable hyperparameters that adjust the balance between safety and efficiency.

##### **OmicsBench: a long-horizon multi-turn benchmark of AI agents on research-study-scale bioinformatics**
2610.21074v1 by Gideon Shaked, Siddharth Nagisetty

We present OmicsBench, a long-horizon, multi-turn bioinformatics benchmark for AI agents. Each of its 97 tasks links dependent subtasks into a directed acyclic graph, for 662 underlying subtasks in all. Each subtask is posed as one user turn and graded deterministically against an answer key, with optional in-context feedback on incorrect answers. Across 12 models on three harnesses, the slowest agent averages 3.6 hours per task, and no combination comes near saturating the benchmark, with mean scores of 0.43 to 0.57 out of a possible 1 without in-context feedback and 0.54 to 0.67 with it. Multi-turn evaluation also uncovers behavior that single-shot prompts cannot, as agents score lower on subtasks that depend on earlier subtasks, and in-context feedback helps agents recover some of that loss. Because deterministic scoring gives no qualitative account of how an agent failed, we also introduce an unsupervised failure mode discovery pipeline that describes, embeds, and clusters agents' failed attempts to surface emergent failure modes. ##### **Athanor: An Agent-Native System for Autonomous Post-Training Research**
2610.21055v1 by Yufa Zhou, Yichen Wang, Jiakun Fan, Ziqi Shu, Zhichao Liu, Anru R. Zhang, Neiwen Ling, Zijun Wei

Recursive self-improvement, in which AI systems build stronger AI systems, is one promising route to superintelligence. We study a weaker form that can be measured today: frontier agents post-training a small raw base model with public data, as a test of their research potential. We build Athanor, an open agent-native system for autonomous post-training research (AutoPostTrain) on real GPUs. We give the agents full research autonomy, low-friction engineering tools, and efficient starter code. The agents carry out the research themselves: they read datasets and evaluation results, build their training data, write and modify training algorithms, tune hyperparameters, and analyze the results to decide what to try next. They work in a sandbox and cannot use external teacher models. Athanor's services supply frozen evaluation, managed jobs, decontaminated data, a persistent research record, and a held-out suite the agents are unaware of. In a 104-hour campaign, a team of four Claude Opus 5.5 agents, each on an eight-GPU node and given no research advice, improves Qwen3-4B-Base from 15.9 to 67.3 on a six-task development suite and from 9.2 to 48.2 on a held-out suite, against 66.5 and 47.9 for the official Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507. ##### **INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples**
2610.21038v1 by Sara Rezaeimanesh, Chad Klochko, Kundan Thind, Mohammad Ghassemi

Machine learning models for tabular prediction typically assume that training data are representative of the population on which they will be deployed. In practice, however, data are often collected based on convenience, availability, or cost, resulting in systematic sampling biases that can distort feature--target relationships and undermine real-world generalization. Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. The benchmark comprises 200 tabular classification and regression datasets spanning diverse application domains and generates around 15,500 shifted training distributions which lead to substantial downstream degradation.

##### **SkillNEO: Non-Elitist Evolutionary Optimization of Agent Skills**
2610.20926v1 by Enze Ma, Chunyu Miao, Yankai Chen, Jie Yang, Liancheng Fang, Kening Zheng, Hanrong Zhang, Muyao Xu, Xinle Yu, Chengze Li, Wei-Chieh Huang, Ziwei Ren, Yunhao Du, Zhixiang Shen, Xue Liu, Philip S. Yu, Zhen Wang

Agent skills turn procedural knowledge into reusable artifacts, allowing LLM agents to accumulate and refine expertise through experience. Building on this, skill evolution iteratively improves skills using execution feedback. However, improvement may require several interdependent edits, so low-reward versions can still be worth developing. Requiring immediate reward gains can discard useful partial repairs before their benefits emerge. Non-elitist evolution allows new versions to receive further edits despite scoring below earlier ones. For agent skills, this calls for evidence of what to preserve and repair next. We introduce SkillNEO (Non-Elitist Evolutionary Optimization for Agent Skills), which separately selects a version to use and versions to keep improving. Comparing how related versions perform the same task reveals working steps to preserve and remaining failures to fix. It can also revisit and revise earlier versions using later evidence. Across 86 SkillsBench tasks in eight domains, including software engineering and office work, SkillNEO raises GPT-5.4 Mini's average reward from 49.1% to 66.4%, within GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6 Luna's original-skill range. Mini executes tasks, proposes revisions, and edits skills; final evaluation uses the same task instances. Gains span all eight domains. The same skills improve stronger models without further editing. On the 41 tasks with modified skills, reuse improves Luna by 10.5 percentage points and places GPT-5.5 10.6 points above its published original-skill reference. Execution records show these skills can benefit stronger models even when Mini still fails some task requirements. Search histories also show lower-scoring versions leading to skills ultimately selected for use. Together, these findings show that skill evolution can turn a weaker model's partial progress into reusable procedural knowledge for improving more capable agents.

2610.20832v1 by Anita Kriz, Antonin Berthon, Nicolás Astorga, Mihaela van der Schaar

Despite their extensive pretraining knowledge and strong reasoning capabilities, large language models (LLMs) struggle to adaptively gather information in experimental design tasks. Our approach specifies the experimental generative model independently of the LLM's own beliefs, allowing us to optimize expected information gain under a model of our choosing. We investigate the design choices for amortization, including alternative generative-model constructions, reward estimation, hypothesis-space specification, multi-step objectives, and computational costs relative to training- and inference-time BED baselines. On Twenty Questions, the resulting policies improve information acquisition and task performance, outperform inference- and training-time BED baselines at substantially lower computational cost, and generalize to unseen categories. These results suggest that information gathering can be learned as a generalizable reasoning skill in LLMs.

We present Agent Runtime Inspection and Amendment (ARIA), a framework for learning when and how to repair ongoing multi-agent executions. From these trajectories, we construct the ARIA dataset, to our knowledge the first large-scale dataset pairing failed multi-agent trajectories with execution-verified repairs. It contains 3{,}953 verified repair pairs, each specifying what went wrong, where to intervene, and how execution should continue. Then, we develop the ARIA framework for MAS-agnostic runtime inspection and amendment, designed to integrate with any MAS that exposes execution trajectories and supports continuation replacement. Using verified repairs as training examples, we teach a compact model to recognize when intervention is needed and how to correct an ongoing run. ##### **Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets**
2610.20587v1 by Shubhendu Trivedi, Jacob Eisenstein, Alekh Agarwal, Ian Gemp, Michael Kaisers, Adam Fisch

An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.
