Large language models (LLMs) increasingly tackle complex real-world tasks in specialized domains. However, identifying and correctly applying domain-specific rules remains challenging when scenario information is distributed across multiple files. Existing rule-reasoning benchmarks typically represent scenarios using only a few factual statements, simplifying the identification and application of relevant rules. To address this gap, this paper introduces \textbf{RuleTrek}, a multi-domain benchmark for agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios. Its rule-grounded construction pipeline extracts structured rule graphs, generates and incrementally composes rule-specific sub-scenarios while ensuring consistency and solvability. To systematically evaluate agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios, RuleTrek assesses three capabilities in interactive workspaces: Rule Identification (RI), Rule Deduction (RD), and End-to-End Rule Reasoning (E2E). Experiments on 11 frontier LLMs show that the highest RI and RD scores are only 62.47\% and 60.43\%, respectively, while E2E scores range from 26.97\% to 55.29\%. These results demonstrate that current LLM agents face substantial challenges in rule reasoning over complex scenarios. We make our code and dataset publicly available at \href{https://github.com/yuan247/RuleTrek}{RuleTrek}.

##### **Interlock: From Local Correctness to Task Completion in Long-Horizon Agents**
2610.19686v1 by Linji Hao, Yanji He, Zihan Wang, Hansi Yang, Jiayuan Zhang, Hongyan Hao, Qi Gu, Yan Feng, Han Wu

Large language model agents increasingly undertake long-horizon tasks in complex environments characterized by fragmented evidence, partially completed states, and dependencies across resources and artifacts. Existing agents may satisfy individual requirements while failing to maintain these relationships, producing locally correct but incomplete outcomes. These failures arise because task completion depends on relationships among environmental evidence, consequential decisions, and required effects. To address this completion gap, we propose Interlock, a framework for task synthesis that represents each task as a typed completion graph. Grounding edges connect public evidence to decisions, coupling hyperedges impose joint constraints across components, and realization edges connect decisions to all required downstream effects. Interlock compiles this graph into a resettable workspace, a public request, and an evaluation rubric. A source-coverage review checks whether the original requirements survive generation, while a public-input review checks whether the rubric is supported by the materials available to the solver. An agent-as-judge inspects execution artifacts and runs verification scripts, retaining trajectories judged to satisfy all mandatory requirements. To evaluate training utility, we fine-tune two Qwen3.6 backbones on their own accepted trajectories. Across the two backbones, training improves AutomationBench task pass rates by 4.00-4.50 percentage points, JobBench weighted scores by 3.20-6.64 points, and Harvey LAB criterion pass rates by 3.89-4.16 percentage points. These results demonstrate the training utility of the synthesized experience across external task environments.

##### **Learning When to Trust Medical Knowledge: Regression-Safe Routing for Clinical Reasoning**
2610.19663v1 by Hui Zhang, Yujia Zeng, Yuhao Xin

Clinical reasoning LLMs need external medical knowledge, but retrieved evidence and reasoning chains can be outdated, poisoned, or hallucinated. Harmful knowledge can cause regression: changing a correct answer into an incorrect one. We construct a medical knowledge-poisoning benchmark covering retrieved evidence and reasoning chains, with seven attack families filtered by a three-judge "subtle-but-wrong" vote and paired clean exams that separate poisoning effects from question difficulty. No fixed knowledge-use strategy is robust across these settings: strong RAG methods trade robustness to poisoned evidence against accuracy when evidence is useful. We formulate knowledge use as per-instance routing and introduce Med-TrustRouter, a lightweight controller that keeps the solver frozen. From leakage-free runtime signals, it estimates each action's expected correctness gain and uncertainty, preserving the base answer unless another action is confidently better. Across seven medical reasoning settings, Med-TrustRouter achieves 78.9 macro and 69.2 worst-case accuracy, matching the strongest competitor on macro accuracy while substantially improving the worst-case floor. Its gains transfer across solvers and evidence judges, unlike a hand-coded router using the same signals. Similar performance across linear, tree, and neural action-value heads suggests that the benefit comes from routing rather than estimator complexity. These results support learning when to trust external knowledge for robust clinical reasoning.

##### **HACHIMI-II: From Role-Play to Learner Behavior Modeling for Faithful Student Simulation**
2610.19654v1 by Yilin Jiang, Fei Tan, Xuanyu Yin, Jing Leng, Aimin Zhou

Simulated students let researchers test tutoring systems and teaching strategies before real learners are available, but only if the simulation reproduces where a particular learner succeeds and fails. The common approach asks a large language model to role-play a student, but a role does not model what this learner knows or will do next, so the model's own competence fills the gap. Simulated students perform at a level set by the model, differ little from one another, and seldom make the real learners' errors. We model the learner's behavior and recast student simulation as third-person prediction of the learner's next action. HACHIMI-II builds a student model from the learner's history, infers the learner's state for the current item or tutor turn, and compiles it into a behavior harness that constrains the next action. On three item-response datasets, the Wasserstein-1 distance between simulated and real student ability is 2.4--8.4$\times$ smaller than that of the strongest baseline. On two dialogue datasets, HACHIMI-II reproduces real student errors with the same knowledge type, locus, and mechanism 1.8--5.1$\times$ more often than the strongest baseline, and ablations trace the gains mainly to the explicit learner state. Predicting the learner makes capable models usable as simulated students.

##### **AWARE-RG: Abstraction Aware Explanation of Resiliency Graphs for Cyber Physical Systems**
2610.19650v1 by Shadaab Kawnain Bashir, Rakesh Podder, Philip D. Hopkins, Sarath Sreedharan, Indrakshi Ray, Indrajit Ray

Understanding the security posture of cyber physical systems (CPS) requires reasoning across both information technology (IT) and operational technology (OT). However, a security analyst often holds an incomplete, role-specific view of the underlying system which has most likely resulted from their knowledge limited by a need-to-know framework. Such an incomplete view can potentially lead them to incorrectly assess if a cyber attack produces an untoward physical consequence resulting in making uninformed decisions about system resilience. In this work, we present AWARE-RG (Abstraction Aware Explanation of Resiliency Graphs), a semi-automated, human-in-the-loop framework that provides an analyst with a role-bounded, need-to-know explanations of resiliency paths in a CPS. The AWARE-RG abstraction structure is derived using Formal Concept Analysis over MITRE ATT&CK for ICS tactics, ordered by the ICS Cyber Kill Chain. For valid attack-fault queries, plan repair and backward means-end analysis identify the missing dependencies, explain why the physical hazard is reachable, and suggest defensive strategies. For invalid attack-fault queries, AWARE-RG localizes the first abstraction level at which the path fails, and reports confirmed blocking conditions as hardening pointers.

##### **Sparse Autoencoders Are a Shared Coordinate System for Symbolic Matching**
2610.19636v1 by Xiaocong Yang, Xuanyu Meng, Yuji Zhang, Jiawei Han, ChengXiang Zhai

Large language models (LLMs) hold broad knowledge in one set of weights, but lack interpretability and trustworthy reasoning, especially in high-stakes use cases. A neuro-symbolic model, with its interpretable architecture and System 2 reasoning capacity, is a promising alternative if built at the scale of LLMs; however, its symbolic backbone is usually constructed with human effort, so existing neuro-symbolic models are bottlenecked by the scope of the symbol component and usually domain-specific. A data-driven method could instead build a larger symbolic system by aligning and merging existing ones. We target its first, foundational step: matching units across these heterogeneous systems. Our method, SAE-IDF, encodes a symbol in its native context into a sparse code with a sparse autoencoder (SAE), re-weights each dimension by its rarity and matches across systems. On 12 tasks across 6 kinds of symbolic system, SAE-IDF outperforms the same LLM's dense baseline on all tasks with Gemma-2-9B and all but one with Llama-3.1-8B, by up to 45 points, consistently across SAE layers and configurations. SAE-IDF is also interpretable: every dimension of the sparse code has a human-readable meaning, so each match decomposes into named concepts (features). Our work paves the way to a universal symbolic backbone.

##### **Are We Really Measuring Forgetting? Pitfalls in Evaluating Machine Unlearning for Multimodal LLMs**
2610.19613v1 by Oscar Chew, Po-Yi Lu, Yi-Jie Cheng, Kunal Jain, Hsuan-Tien Lin, Zhengzhong Tu, Kuan-Hao Huang

As machine unlearning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) rapidly advances, rigorous evaluation of its effectiveness becomes increasingly important. However, existing benchmarks largely inherit evaluation protocols from text-only unlearning and overlook the unique challenges of unlearning in multimodal settings. In this work, we identify three major pitfalls that may lead to misleading conclusions: the lack of heldout evaluation to distinguish genuine concept-wise forgetting from instance-wise overfitting, the reliance on synthetic, fine-tuned knowledge rather than real-world knowledge acquired during pre-training, and unfair method comparisons caused by applying the same training settings to different unlearning methods. To address these challenges, we introduce GEMMU (Generalizable Evaluation for Multimodal Machine Unlearning), a benchmark built on real-world knowledge and a principled evaluation protocol. Re-evaluating representative unlearning methods under GEMMU reveals substantial generalization gaps and challenges conclusions drawn from previous benchmarks. Our findings highlight the importance of rigorous evaluation for advancing multimodal machine unlearning and provide guidelines for future benchmark design.

##### **PA1-OPD: One-Step Prefix-Augmented On-Policy Distillation for Language Model Pretraining**
2610.19601v1 by Zhenhe Wu, Jian Yang, Yaping Jin, Xinlai Kang, Jie Hu, Hanting Chen

Knowledge distillation (KD) improves language-model pretraining by matching teacher distributions at corpus prefixes, but provides no direct supervision at contexts induced by student predictions. We introduce PA1-OPD, which augments corpus-path distillation with teacher feedback after one student-sampled transition per selected prefix. This expands the supervised states while retaining next-token prediction and corpus-path KD. Data-anchored roots support parallel sampling, and differentiable tree attention shares prefix computation without changing inference. In two continued-pretraining settings, full coverage improves held-out cross-entropy over KD across C4, PG-19, and Pile at every evaluated checkpoint. In Stage2, selecting the 10% of roots with highest student surprisal adds 48.85% to KD's Macro cross-entropy gain over next-token training. Gains remain when branch supervision excludes successor states already on the corpus path, supporting the value of feedback beyond observed contexts. Additional depth or breadth improves aggregate results at unchanged per-term supervision weight, with benefits varying by corpus. These findings support local student-induced supervision as a complement to pretraining distillation.

##### **XferAgent: Cross-Agent Experience Transfer for Self-Improving LLM Agents**
2610.19581v1 by Weihao Li, Jun Bai, Shijie Chen, Qincheng Lu, Xinyu Wang, Ziyang Song

Large language model (LLM) agents can reuse experience from prior task executions to improve performance on subsequent tasks. Although existing methods enable cross-agent experience sharing, differences in agents' execution histories and task requirements make shared experiences difficult to reuse effectively. We propose XferAgent, a framework for cross-agent experience transfer that organizes reusable experience in local and shared experience graphs. XferAgent reconciles compatible contributions from heterogeneous agents while avoiding unreliable merges. For each new task, it selects shared experiences and integrates them with the target agent's local experience to support task execution. Experiments across diverse task domains show improvements over representative memory-based methods and effective experience transfer between heterogeneous agent frameworks. Cumulative task success also increases over successive task passes as agents update and reuse their experiences.

##### **Learning Across Temporal Graph Representation Silos**
2610.19576v1 by Minwoo Yu, Young-guk Ha

Temporal graph data are recorded in different representations, but whether these differences also determine the scope of learned knowledge reuse remains unclear. This study investigates whether predictive structure learned from relational histories can be reused between continuous-time dynamic graphs (CTDGs) and temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs). Bidirectional reuse emerges while retaining distinct input and prediction interfaces and independent vocabularies, and sources with the same representation are not always more useful. Controlled reorganizations of source histories show that the organization contributing to reuse varies across source-target conditions. We show that learned aspects of Relational Dynamics, the predictive structure present in relational histories, can be reused across conventional representation boundaries. Independently pretrained TKG and CTDG learners can also be fused at the score level to improve prediction on a third dataset without entity or relation-semantic alignment. Two proofs of concept in recommendation and security detection illustrate that external temporal sources without direct alignment can also become useful pretraining resources.

##### **Divergence-Routed On-Policy Distillation for Language Models**
2610.19572v1 by Tiantian Peng, Guannan Lv, Hongyang Li, Qiuhe Hong, Hang Cheng, Hongjian Dou, Xiangyu Wu, Yuyang Liu

On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers knowledge from a teacher model to a student along student-generated trajectories, typically by minimizing the reverse Kullback-Leibler (RKL) divergence. However, RKL can provide weak corrective signals for teacher-supported tokens to which the student assigns very low probability. This raises two questions: which positions are most consequential for the behavioral gap between the student and teacher, and what additional supervision is needed at these positions? Through cross-sampling, we find that divergence-based routing more effectively pinpoints positions contributing to the teacher-student performance gap than entropy-based routing. Further analysis reveals teacher-mode under-coverage at high Jensen-Shannon (JS) divergence positions, where standard OPD increases the large teacher-student probability gaps. Motivated by these findings, we propose Divergence-Routed On-Policy Distillation (DrOPD). DrOPD retains RKL supervision at all positions and selectively adds forward Kullback-Leibler (FKL) divergence supervision at positions with high JS divergence. Experiments across six math benchmarks show that DrOPD improves over standard OPD and entropy-based routing. DrOPD also reduces severe under-coverage cases by 37.9 percent relative to OPD, supporting targeted FKL as an effective complement to OPD.

##### **TEX-MEX: Similarity-Guided Temporal Contrastive Learning for Time Series**
2610.19557v1 by Aditya Radhakrishnan, Maxwell A. Xu, Wanting Mao, Santosh Kumar, Harish Haresamudram, James M. Rehg

Contrastive learning for time series depends on deciding which pairs of inputs should be treated as similar and dissimilar. Because few augmentations reliably preserve meaning in time series, prior methods have relied on two disjoint notions of similarity between windows: structural similarity, which relates windows with similar morphology, and temporal similarity, which relates windows that occur close together in time. We introduce TEX-MEX, to our knowledge the first approach to unify the two into a single relationship. Given a random batch of windows drawn across the dataset, TEX-MEX uses the encoder to identify which windows are structurally similar or dissimilar to each other. It then pulls each window toward the temporally shifted versions of similar windows and pushes it away from those of dissimilar ones, learning what typically accompanies similar windows in time. Across PPG, EEG, ECG, accelerometry, and continuous glucose monitoring, TEX-MEX broadly achieves state-of-the-art performance against contrastive, reconstructive, JEPA-based, and modality-specific approaches, establishing it as a strong general SSL objective. We further train a 560K-parameter PPG foundation model that achieves state-of-the-art performance across a broad range of downstream benchmarks while being roughly 50x smaller than the next-best unimodally pretrained PPG foundation model in our evaluation. We will release this foundation model open-source and open-weight to the community to support future work and practical applications.

##### **Evidence-Anchored Overdose Death Prediction from Death Investigation Reports**
2610.19556v1 by Raavi Gupta, Ziqing Yang, Karli R. Hochstatter, Talia Nadel, Nicole D'Anna, Jason K. Graham, Hannah Johnson, Kelsey Opozda, Nabila El-Bassel, Anubhav Jangra, Smaranda Muresan

Confirmation of overdose deaths in the United States is often delayed due to the complexities of certifying drug-involved deaths. Death investigation reports represent a rich but underutilized data source that could be used to predict whether a death is a suspected overdose (i.e., a death preliminarily identified as an overdose prior to the final cause and manner of death determination). We present an automated framework that classifies suspected overdoses from death investigation reports, while producing supporting explanations guided by expert knowledge. Through an expert-guided design process, we investigate 1) what type of explanations are best for model prediction performance, and 2) which architecture is better suited for this task: pipeline models that rationalize first and then predict, or self-rationalizing models that jointly predict and rationalize? Our experiments show that a pipeline model with explanations grounded in the input death investigation reports outperforms the other settings. Our best model also outperforms a rule-based system currently in deployment by 15%, while allowing expert interventions for final decisions.

##### **LabCompiler: A Framework for Verifiable Human-Machine Laboratory Execution from Imperfect Protocols**
2610.19550v1 by Jingyuan Li, Dongzhan Zhou, Minting Pan, Yujie Liu, Yuan Gao, Yankai Jiang, Xinyu Gu, Zhe Liu, Yang Li, Bo Zhang, Lei Bai, Tong Si, Weinan Zhang

Closing the loop of AI-driven science requires reliably translating natural-language protocols into executable laboratory workflows despite protocol defects and heterogeneous hardware. Existing approaches often couple scientific interpretation with device-specific code generation, making errors difficult to localize and limiting reuse across laboratory platforms. We introduce LabCompiler, a robust protocol-to-execution framework that transforms imperfect natural-language protocols into verifiable human-machine workflows. Its protocol firewall diagnoses and repairs defects before compilation while preserving unaffected content, guided by troubleshooting knowledge accumulated through perturbation-guided self-evolution. A two-level, device-agnostic graph representation separates experimental semantics from hardware-specific implementation and supports capability-based device assignment with coordinated human execution. We further introduce a protocol recovery benchmark covering four controlled perturbation types and naturally occurring errors collected from protocols written by iGEM participants. Experiments show that LabCompiler outperforms frontier LLMs in protocol error correction while enabling coordinated human-machine execution across heterogeneous, partially automated laboratory settings.
